Third Middlesex Jury, before Mr. Baron Bolland.
137. STEPHEN HANNIBELL and GEORGE PRESTON were indicted for feloniously breaking and entering the dwelling-house of Sophia Savage, on the 12th of November, at St. Mary Abbotts, Kensington, and stealing therein, 3 counterpanes, value 30s.; 1 reticule, value 40s.; 2 waistcoats, value 10s.; 4 yards of silk, value 7s.; 1 shirt, value 2s.; 4 forks, value 4s.; 1 handkerchief, value 6d.; 1 skirt of a gown, value 3s.; and 1 pair of breeches, value 3s.; her goods.
SARAH COTTLE. I am servant to Mrs. Maxwell, of Lyndon-grove, Kensington; I know Mrs. Sophia Savage; her house is No. 2, Holland Park Terrace, in the parish of Kensington - she sleeps at Mrs. Maxwell's - I have been in the habit of going to Mrs. Savage's house from eight o'clock in the morning, until four o'clock in the afternoon, to act as a servant there - I have lived at Mrs. Maxwell's twelve months, and Hannibell has been in the habit of going backwards and forwards with messages for her - he was employed in moving for her from Berners-street, to Holland Park Terrace, to Mrs. Savage's, about twelve months ago - he has cleaned knives at Mrs. Savage's; he was employed there last, about six weeks ago - he asked me once if anybody slept in the house, and I told him there was a policeman; I cannot say whether that was the last time he was employed there or before - I cannot say when it was - I do not recollect his asking me that more than once - on the 12th of November, I was at the house; I fastened and bolted all the doors and windows, and left at four o'clock in the afternoon - I left nobody in it - nobody slept there at that time - I locked the outer door and went away - the policeman came round at two o'clock and in consequence of his information, I went to the house at two o'clock on Wednesday morning; I found two policemen in the house - I found the flap of the coal cellar up - it was fixed down in the earth when I was there the day before - I found the drawing room windows opened - I had left those windows safe - one flag stone round the cellar grating had been lifted up - there is a grating in the middle of the stone; it was large enough to admit a person to go down it - I went up stairs to the first floor bed room, and missed a counterpane, and two counterpanes from the next room; I went up stairs and found a trunk broken open, and missed the skirt of a merino dress, and a reticule, two waistcoats, a piece of figured silk, a shawl, and a pair of small clothes - mistress had opened her trunk in my presence, on the 12th of November, and I saw them - she took the things out and I put them in again - the trunk was locked; it was left safe - I have seen these things since at Marylebone station-house - I missed some forks next morning the 14th of November, from a drawer in the china closet - I did not examine the drawer until then - they were there on the 12th of November - Mrs. Sophia Savage is a widow - she is at Bath - she lives at No. 7, Lyndon-grove, with Mrs. Maxwell her mother; but she had this house in Holland Park Terrace - she lived there for a week or fortnight only - there has been a family living in the house - it is a house to be let - she has been out of town two or three months.
Cross-examined by MR. PHILLIPS. Q. Mrs. Savage has been away for the last two months? A. Yes; two months from the time we missed the things; she was not living there on the 12th of November - nobody lived in the house - she told me herself these were her own things - her christian name is Sophia - I only know that from what mistress tells me - her husband has been dead two years and a half - I never saw Mr. Savage.
HENRY GARNET. (policeman). On the evening of the 12th of November, I met the two prisoners in Upper Seymour-street; they both had bundles; it was about ten minutes before ten o'clock - Preston went up to a door in Seymour-street and said to Hannibell, "This is No. 47;" I crossed over on the other side, and followed them down the street, into Portman-street; one went on one side of the street, and the other on the other; when they came to Oxford-street, Hannibell kept on the left hand, and Preston went on the right; I saw McLean speak to him; I then crossed over and asked Hannibell where he had come from? he said, "From Notting-hill;" Mrs. Savage's house is at Notting-hill; I asked where he was going to - he said, "To the Tower," - I asked if he knew the young man who was with him just now with the bundle - he said, he did and called out "George"- I said, we would go over to him, and not call him over; we went over to him and McLean had him in custody; we took them down to the watch-house, and in the way to the watch-house, I asked Hannibell what he had got there - he said, he did not know - I asked where he got them from - he said, he found them in Edgware-road; I had not then opened the bundles; when I got to the watch-house, I opened the bundles and found one counterpane in a handkerchief, and a counterpane in an apron in Hannibell's bundle; there was nothing else in his bundle; I found a flint steel, a match, and two keys on Hannibell; one of them was a latch key, the other a common box key; I also found on him a bag; it dropped from under his clothes, while I was searching him; there was nothing in it - that is all I found on him - I saw the other bundle opened - there was a reticule in it, also two waistcoats, a counterpane, a pair of small clothes, a piece of figured silk, a shirt, and the skirt of a merino gown.
Cross-examined. Q. What kind of night was it? A. Very foggy; I do not remember whether it had been wet; the roads were not dry - there was a damp from the fog - there were a number of link-boys in Oxford-street; I did not go further; I believe it was such a fog a man might have been one hundred yards off with a link and I not see him - I do not know what parish the house is in.
DONALD McLEAN (Policeman). On the evening of the 12th of November, I stopped Preston in Oxford-street,he had a bundle, I asked what he had got, he said, a counterpane; I asked him where he had brought it from, he said, from Kensington gravel-pits; I asked where he was going, he said, to his sister's, in St. John-square, Clerkenwell - I took him into custody, Garnett and Hannibell joined me; and when I got to the station-house, I opened the bundle, it contained a pair of small-clothes, two waistcoats, one shift, a shirt, a piece of figured silk, a counterpane, and a reticule bag - I found four plated forks in his pocket.
WILLIAM COWLE (Policeman). I know Mrs. Savage's house. On the night of the 12th of November, between twelve and one o'clock, I was called to the house, and found it had been entered, by a stone being removed in front of the door; it was a square stone, with an iron plate in the middle, it was the plate of the coalcellar - there was quite room enough for either of the prisoners to get down - the coal-cellar is not more than a yard deep - I found the drawing-room window open - the inner-doors of the house were open, they were not broken - I went up stairs, and in the back bed-room, I found a trunk broken open; the drawers in the house were also open.
SARAH COTTLE. I know this reticule; there is a note in it, which was taken out, and laid on the ground - these counterpanes I had folded up the day before - all the things were taken out of the house that night - I know the reticule well, Mrs. Savage never uses it; it was a present from a lady.
Cross-examined. Q. Do you know what Mrs. Savage's husband was? A. A physician; the house in Berner's-street is the house they used to live in when Dr. Savage was living; she has never lived there since he died; she lived at her mother's - there were two bills put up to let the house in Holland-park.
Hannibell's Defence. On the 12th of November, we were lighting a gentleman's horse and chaise, down Notting-hill. I kicked against the bundle. I said to Preston, "George, here is a bundle;" we looked about and found another bundle, and four forks.
Lucy Ralph, of Uxbridge; Mary Preston, Kensington Gravel-pits; Amelia Abel, Kensington Gravel-pits; Mary Anderson, Camden-place, Kensington Gravel-pits; gave the prisoners a good character.
HANNIBELL - GUILTY. Aged 19.
PRESTON - GUILTY. Aged 21. - Of Stealing Only.
Transported Seven Years.
[trialAccount t18331128-133]
138. JEREMIAH BORRETT was indicted for feloniously secreting and embezzling on the 11th of October, a certain letter, addressed to Dennis Newberry, No. 6, Grafton-court, North-street, Manchester-square, and containing 1 sovereign; which came into his hands and possession, whilst in the employ of the General Postoffice.
9 OTHER COUNTS varying the manner of laying the charge.
MESSRS. ADOLPHUS, SHEPHERD, and SCARLETT, conducted the prosecution.
CECILIA ROUND. I am nurse in Lord Jermyn's family, and was so in October - I was then at Ickworth, near Bury, with his Lordship's family - Margaret Newberry was the wet-nurse - I wrote a letter for her in October, I folded it up, and put a sovereign in it, and sewed it into the letter, to come under the seal - I received the sovereign from Margaret Newberry - I sewed it into the letter; I sealed it - I wrote a direction on a slip of paper; it was "Mr. Newberry, 6, Grafton-court, North-street, Manchester-square" - I gave the letter and slip of paper to Margaret Newberry - I don't recollect on what day- it was some time in the afternoon.
Cross-examined by MR. STAMMERS. Q. Are you sure you wrote No. 6, Grafton-court, and not Grafton-street? A. Yes; I dated it in October I know; about the 8th or 9th.
MARGARET NEWBERRY. I am wet-nurse to Lord Jermyn. I desired Cecilia Round to write a letter, and gave her a sovereign to be inclosed in it - I recollect receiving the letter from her when written; there was a direction with it - I gave the letter and direction to the footman, Richard Pope, to get Lord Jermyn to frank it; I desired it to be directed to my husband, D. Newberry, 6, Grafton-court, North-street, Manchester-square - I have never seen it since, nor the money.
Cross-examined. Q. How long have you been married? A. In 1829 or 1830; I am not sure which; I can read - it was directed to Dennis Newberry; I remember that, because his lordship sent to know the Christian name; it was somewhere about the beginning of October - I saw Cecilia Round sew the sovereign in the letter; I was there all the time.
RICHARD POPE. I am in service of Lord Jermyn, and was so in October last. I remember in that month the witness gave me a letter for his Lordship to frank; it was about the 10th, or thereabout - she gave me a slip of paper with the address, which I remember very well; it was "Mr. Newberry, 3, (or 6,) Grafton-court, Manchester-square, London;" I don't know whether it was three or six; no street was mentioned on the slip of paper- I took the letter to his Lordship's dressing-room, and laid it on the table with other letters, to be franked; his Lordship was not there - his Lordship could not frank the letter for that day, but on the following day, when he sat down to frank it, he sent me into the nursery, to inquire Newberry's Christian name, and the answer I received from Mrs. Newberry was, Dennis Newberry; I communicated that to his Lordship, and to the best of my belief it was so directed - there was a locked box, in which letters for the Post-office were deposited; the steward had the key of it; there was but one key - the letters are taken out of the box by the steward, and deposited in a bag, which bag has a lock to it also; the steward keeps the key of the bag, and the postmaster at Bury keeps another key of it - I took the letter from his Lordship, with other letters, and put it into the letterbox, which was in the entrance-hall; I did not notice whether there was anything in it.
Cross-examined. Q. How long have you been in Earl Jermyn's service? A. Two years next February - I recollect being questioned about the letter very shortly afterwards, by the nurse - that was about five or six days afterwards; I think it was about the 15th that I was questioned about it; it might possibly be a day or twolater - I speak with confidence of the direction; it was,"Mr. Newberry, 3, (or 6), Grafton-court, Manchester-square, London" - that was the address on the slip of paper; no street was mentioned on the paper - I did not take particular notice of the address, but I am certain Dennis Newberry was written on the letter, because I saw his Lordship do it - North-street was not on the letter - I am not certain whether it was Grafton-court or street, I took particular notice of the direction; the word "Grafton" was on it; the word "North" was not on the letter I am certain; I am not certain whether the number was three or six - I took the letter to the letterbox in the entrance-hall - when I first received it, I immediately took it to Lord Jermyn's dressing-room, and left it there on the table - any of the servants might go up into that room; his lordship's establishment consists of about thirty persons, when we are altogether; Lord Jermyn has in the house four servants of his own; there are about twenty-six in, and about the premises - the two housemaids and myself have access to the drawing-room; the door is not locked - I did not examine the letter with a view to ascertain whether there was any thing in it or not - I could not see whether there was any money in it - there is only one key to the box, which the steward has the charge of - I dropped the letter in at the top of the box - the steward could open the box at his pleasure.
JOHN ARSCOTT. I was Lord Bristol's house-steward, and was at Ickworth, in October - there is a box for the letters which has a lock and key - I kept the key - there was only one key to my knowledge - if a letter was put into it and required to be taken out again, I was applied to to open the box - on the 9th and 10th of October, I believe, I took the letters out of the box to put into the bag for the post; it was my duty to do so; I put them into a bag to be sent to the post-office, at Bury St. Edmunds - that bag had a lock and key; there were two keys to it - I kept one, and the post-master at Bury St. Edmunds, kept the other - I locked the bag on those days and did so always - I gave the bag to a person kept on purpose to take it to the post-office.
Cross-examined. Q. How long have you left Lord Bristol's service? A. About a month - why I left the service is a matter between his lordship and myself - I will swear it does not arise from the present affair - I knew nothing of the present affair until last week, and I dare say I could produce testimonials from his lordship; I am now in his lordship's service at another place - I have no other reason to remember this particularly, except that it was a duty conferred on me, and I know I performed it - I took the letters out of the box that day; I cannot say how many there were; it is quite useless to try to remember. I believe Lord Bristol is a Member of the House of Peers; his lordship's letters generally come directed to himself - the number of letters vary from five or six to fifteen or sixteen a day; it depended entirely on what company was in the house - I have no recollection how many there were on the 9th or 10th of October - I have sometimes examined the addresses, if a party has wished to know if a frank had been put into the box for them - I cannot swear whether I examined the letters on the evenings of the 9th or 10th of October; I sent the bag to the post-office by Ralph who assists in the stables and fetches and carries the letters; he generally takes them; he always brings me the box - I cannot say whether he took them or not; I have sent them by another person once; I only remember doing so once.
MR. SHEPHERD. Q. Did you dispatch the letters from the box up to the end of October? A. I did.
LORD JERMYN. I recollect franking a letter for Margaret Newberry, about the 10th of October - it ought to have been directed to Grafton-court, for I went to the place myself to engage the wet nurse, and knew she lived there - I recollect the person's name I directed it to was Dennis Newberry, and I should have thought that I directed it to Grafton-court, North-street, Manchester-square, but I may have made a mistake - the impression on my mind is not sufficient to say whether I took any particular notice of the letters - I cannot say whether I found it in my dressing-room or not - I generally found them on the table of my dressing-room - when I came to dress in the evening my servant generally asked me if there was any frank to be given away - after I franked letters, I gave them to my servant to put into the box; and the impression on my mind is that I left them in the room; while he was out of the room - I recollect sending him out of the room for the Christian name - it would be Pope's duty to put the letters into the box.
Cross-examined. Q. Is your lordship a member of the House of Peers? A. Of the House of Commons - I sometimes write my full number of letters - I cannot say I recollect on my own knowledge what day of the month it was I directed this letter, but I inquired of my nurses, and they told me it was the day after I returned from an excursion in the country - I referred to a memorandum, and found it was on the 10th - I recollect it by comparing dates - I recollect going away the week after the Bury fair, which I believe was Friday, the 4th; and I think I went on the following Monday, and stayed two nights - I have every reason to believe it was on the 10th of October that I franked the letter - I cannot say I have a distinct recollection of the address I wrote on the letter - I cannot say whether it was Grafton-court, or Grafton-street - I cannot say whether I directed it correctly or not - I know how I ought to have directed it - I cannot say whether North-street was on it or not - I knew at the time that was the place where the person lives - I should say that North-street was probably on the letter - I thought the house was No. 9, but I cannot say, but I believe the number was on the slip of paper which was with the letter - I have not a very distinct recollection of the number; I conceive I copied it from the slip of paper - my impression is that I referred to the slip of paper, for I remember "Mr. Newberry" being on the slip, and thinking it was not the right way to write to a person of his station, I sent to know the Christian name - I had Margaret Newberry's address on a slip of paper, and I once went there to Grafton-court; it is a small place; I should not have called it a street, if I have done so, unless I had been in a hurry.
DANIEL FROST. I am clerk in the office of the Postmaster, at Bury - I have a book, which is kept there; by which, it appears, there was a letter directed to Dennis Newberry, 6, Grafton-street - the entry is my own writing- it was a money-letter; it was taken out of Lord Bristol's letter bag, which came locked to the office; the key of the bag hung up in the office - I unlocked it myself and took the letter out; it was franked; after I entered it, it was laid aside in the box, until the time of making up the letters; and I entered it also on the letter-bill of that day; this is that letter-bill; it was on the 10th of October; here is an entry on the bill, of a money-letter, in the name of Newberry - I make the bill out from the book; they bear the same date; the bill goes up to the post-office, in London, with the letters, to let them know that letter should be there.
Cross-examined. Q. Spell the manner in which you you have written the address? A." D. Newbry;" I always copy the address of money-letters, into this book; and the name, and street, with great correctness and care - I generally copy the Christian name when I copy it at length, and the surname I always copy at length - according to the best of my belief, the entry is a fair copy of the letter - it is Grafton-street, "Grafton" is spelt with one "f" and an "a" - I recollect the letter being franked - my attention was called to the entry, about a month after the 10th of October - I supposed that letter was franked, because it corresponded with the one stated to be lost - (I never could have written "bry" for "berry,") that is the only reason I suppose the letter to have been franked - we always lay the money letters in a small box, made for that purpose; the box was not locked; it was in the centre of the office - master has nine children and a wife in the house, who have access to it; it is a shop, but the office is parted off with a book-shop; there is an entrance from the book-shop into the office - the shop is much frequented - we should see a person passing from the shop to the office; there is no door, but there is an open place to the shop - the postmaster takes the letters out of the box; if he is unwell, his wife, Mrs. Deck, does it, and if she was unwell, I should take them - about four persons in general have access to the box - the post goes out at ten o'clock, the shop is closed at nine o'clock, and then the office duty is done, till the mail stops; the letter arrived on the 10th of October, and was sent to London the same night.
JOHN DECK. I am postmaster, of Bury, St. Edmunds. On the evening of the 10th of October, I did not open the bag which came from Lord Bristol's; a letter bill is forwarded from the office every night, to town, with the letters (looking at a bill) - this signature, John Deck, is mine - I forwarded that bill on the 10th of October, with the letters, as it is now (reads) "10th of October, unpaid letters, for London, 3l. 17s. 6d. unpaid letters passing through London, 16s.; paid letters 1l. 4s.; money-letters, one name Ascroft, anothernamed Newberry"- here is at the bottom of the bill, a direction, that letters containing cash, should be entered, and wrapped up in the bill; and I wrapped up myself, that night, the two letters in the name of Ascroft and Newberry, and forwarded them that night.
Cross-examined. Q. Do you see this book? A. Every night; as the letters are sent off I enter in the book the amount of each bill, the amount of postage from each town, the memorandums are inserted previous to my inserting the amount of postage - on the 10th of October, when I wrote the amount of postage, the memorandums were there - the business of my post-office ought to be conducted with care, and I believe it is; I generally look at the memorandums, containing the name and addresses of the money-letters - I saw that the addresses of the money-letters corresponded with the book, and the letter bill, and I did so on the 10th of October; I discovered no mistake - my clerk opened Lord Bristol's bag - he generally opens it - I have no recollection of his doing so, on the 10th of October - if he is unwell I open it myself - and sometimes part of my family - my family have not assisted me in the business, for months - as regards the bag, I am confident they have not opened it; having examined the bill with the book, I despatch the bill with the moneyletters in it to London - the other money-letter is entered in the bill by the name of Uscott.
CHARLES WILLIAM BARNARD. I am a clerk in the General post-office. On the 11th of October, I was clerk of the money-book - it is my duty to enter in the book such money-letters, as arrived at the post-office, and to receive the letters - a receipt is made out for each letter, which is handed to the letter-carrier, and is brought back to me, and delivered to them - on the 11th of October, I find a money-letter entered "Newberry, Grafton-street, Manchester-square, from Bury, in Suffolk," signed for by J. Borrett, in the hand-writing of the prisoner - (looking at the bill) I received this bill, that morning; here are my initials to it as having received two money-letters - I have another entry, of one in the name of Ascroft, Great Leonard-street, next to it - I have not a doubt that entry was taken from this bill - I delivered the letter addressed to Newberry, to the prisoner.
Cross-examined. Q. How often have you seen the prisoner write? A. Perhaps fifty or one hundred times; I have been in the Post-office for more than four years - I swear that is his signature; I have not the least doubt of it - we are as particular as we can be in making entries in the book; considering the time we have to do it in - we make mistakes sometimes - I have spelled the name "Newbury;" I copied it from the letter, not from the bill - I cannot swear that it was "bury" on the letter; it is "berry" on the bill apparently - I should not think of contracting to "bry" purposely - with the greatest care mistakes will happen; I do not call it a great mistake - we write as carefully as possible.
DENNIS NEWBERRY. My wife is in the service of Lord Jermyn, and was so in October. I live at No. 6, Grafton-court, Paradise-street, Manchester-square. I expected to receive a letter from my wife with money in it, and I never got it - I do not know when it was to come - I wrote to her, when she wrote to me to know why I did not answer this letter, which I had never received - I was sent for to the Post-office afterwards - I have never received a letter from her with a sovereign in it.
Cross-examined. Q. You live in Grafton-court, not street? A. No; there is no Grafton-street in the neighbourhood, that I know of - I have lived in the court two years, and do not know any Grafton-street - either North-street or Paradise-street will do for my address - both streets lead to the court.
MR. ADOLPHUS. Q. Are you a housekeeper or lodger? A. I lodge with Mrs. Jordan.
HANNAH JORDAN. I live at No. 6, Grafton-court, North-street, Manchester-square. Dennis Newberry lodges with me; if the postman brought any letters to my house I always received them while Newberry has been with me - in October last, I received no money-letter for Newberry, nor any letter at all for him - I received no money-letter in October; I know the prisoner; he is the postman who I received letters from when they came - he never delivered me a money-letter in October - he brought a letter for Newberry - I cannot say whether it was in October - I gave Newberry the letter I received.
Cross-examined. Q. Do you know Grafton-street, in your neighbourhood? A. No; I never got but one letter from this woman since she has been at Bury - I never received a letter addressed to Grafton-street - nobody lodged in my room but Newberry - I do not know how many lodge in the house; there are several families lodge in the house - I only occupy one room, which is the parlour on the ground-floor - I do not know about the back room - the two rooms at the top are occupied - I received every letter for Mr. Newberry that ever came to the house, while he was with me - I am not much out of the house, because I have a little family - my husband sells things in the street; I do not go out with him - I have not been out a single day selling fruit within the last three months - I am not the owner of the house; I rent one room, that is all - I never received more than one letter for Mr. Newberry since his wife went into the country.
RICHARD HUNTLEY. I am a letter-carrier. I recollect a short time ago seeing a receipt for a money-letter directed to Grafton-street, North, Manchester-square; I deliver in Grafton-street, Fitzroy-square; it therefore occurred to me that the letter did not belong to my division - I had it in my possession at my seat which is in the same division as the prisoner belongs to - I either gave the receipt to the prisoner, or put it before him; if it had been an unpaid letter I should have given it to the charge taker; but a paid letter or a frank, I should give to the prisoner or put it before him; that would be the course of my duty - when I read the direction, it struck me forcibly at the time it must be intended for Grafton-street, North-street, Manchester-square, and I shewed the receipt to the prisoner; I remember that, and I asked him if that would do for him; he said,"Yes" - I was aware that Grafton-court was in his delivery, I had been on that walk myself some years ago - I remarked to him that that laid wide from my Grafton-street - the prisoner's duty would be to take it into the inner office, and by shewing it to the clerk he would get me letter and sign a book for it; he would take the receipt with the letter to the party it was intended for, and get them to sign it, and he is expected to keep that receipt as his acknowledgement; I have some hundreds of these receipts by me - if a carrier was asked a question about the delivery of a money-letter, he would have the receipt to produce; I have had that to do myself; it has happened to me - the prisoner lives by Chadwells, Gray's-inn-road.
Cross-examined. Q. The letter you saw, and of which you have been speaking, was addressed to Grafton-street, North, Manchester-square? A. I saw no letter; that was the address on the receipt, "Grafton-street, North;" I am pretty sure it was not North-street - I deliver at two Grafton-streets; my delivery is not near Grafton-court; it is the nearest Grafton-street to Grafton-court - the Grafton-street in my delivery, is the nearest Grafton-street to his delivery; his delivery is not next to mine - there is Grafton-street, Fitzroy-square, and Upper Grafton-street; and Pannel, my assistant, delivers in Grafton-street, East.
Q. Suppose you had a letter directed to either of the Grafton-streets in your delivery, should you think of taking it to Grafton-court? A. No; nor should I have sent this, but seeing "North," and "Manchester-square," on it - I either put the receipt before the prisoner where he sat, or gave it into his hand - it is usual at times to put them into the delivery when the carriers are not there it is a frequent custom - I thought it was intended for Grafton-court from its having "North," and "Manchester-square" on it.
MR. ADOLPHUS. Q. When persons are absent from their seats, receipts may be left there for them? A. Yes; the prisoner was at his seat - I will not swear he saw me lay it down; but I said to him, "Will this do for you?" he said, "Yes, it will" - I had his walk once; there is no Grafton-street in that walk; Grafton-court is about half a mile perhaps from the Grafton-street in my walk.
JAMES AUSTIN. I am inspector of letter carriers. I have a book relating to the delivery of letters on the 11th of October; I find an entry of the time the prisoner took to deliver his letters that day; his delivery being in Nottingham place, including Manchester-square, and the court in question; he completed his delivery according to his own signing, at ten minutes after eleven o'clock, on the 11th of October; he was despatched from the office at forty minutes after eight o'clock.
There being no evidence to shew an act of larceny being committed in Middlesex, the prisoner was ACQUITTED.
[trialAccount t18331128-134]
139. JEREMIAH BORRETT was indicted for embezzlement.
ELIZABETH COLLINS. I live at No. 9, Dorset-street, Manchester-square; the prisoner was the letter-carrier in August last; on the 7th of August, he brought me a letter - the postage was 2s. 3d.; it was charged as a treble letter; I opened it while he was there, and it was only double; I told him I thought it was overcharged; he said if I would give him the letter back, he would get the overcharge returned, and bring it to me; I saw him two or three days after, when he came again with a letter or newspaper, and I gave him the letter; I saw him again frequently, but did not ask him for it then; about a fortnight after I gave him the letter, I asked him if the letter and the money were yet returned; he said, "No, Ma'am, I have not yet got it from the office;" I askedhim some time after that again, and he repeated the same answer, that he had not yet got it from the office; I asked him a third time about three weeks or a month before I appeared at Bow-street, which is about three weeks ago; I asked him if he had brought me the letter and money; he said, "No, but I will see about it, and perhaps get it for you to morrow;" he has never given me either the letter or money; when I spoke to him about it, it was at my own house, which is in Middlesex; this is the letter; I am the wife of Robert Collins.
Cross-examined by MR. STAMMERS. Q. What is your husband? A. He keeps an Italian warehouse; he has lived there nearly five years; the letter was brought to me on the 7th of August, I think - I have a particular reason for thinking so, as a servant's character was inclosed in it, and the lady who sent it to me, wished me to give it to the servant who was anxiously expecting the character to arrive; the date of the post-mark is the 7th of August - it came from Lymington; I inquired of him for the overcharge; he always said he had not yet got it.
FRANCIS HARRISON. I am a window clerk in the General Post-office; it is my duty to make the rebate on overcharged letters; I keep a book to enter the letters on which those overcharges are returned - I have the book; here is an entry on the 10th of August, "Collins, No. 9, Dorset-street; the original charge 2s. 3d. - the allowance 1s. 6d.;" I erase the original charge, and put the reduced charge on the letter, and that is done on this letter by myself; it was reduced from a treble, to a single rate; there are other letters as well as this, seven or eight in number, overcharges; the amount of these overcharges is 5s. 6d; when this entry is made, the letters are tied together with a piece of string, and the amount being 5s. 6d., would be placed in that string, merely to secure it from falling out; the letter-carrier's bring them to us in that form, ande we tie them up again and and place the money immediatly under the string; they are returned next morning to the letter-carrier's office; I don't know whose custody that money would come to; my duty don't extend to that department; it is sent to the letter-carrier's office; the receipt of money is acknowledged by a person signing T. or J. Deacon; I don't know who he is; the entry is, "Mrs. Collins, No. 9, Dorset-street, 2s. 3d. - 1s. 6d.; signed, T. Deacon."
Cross-examined. Q. What do you call the rebate? A. An allowance made on letters overcharged; this book contains the entries of the rebates; there is no particular carrier employed for the purpose of taking the letters to the letter-carrier's office: the prisoner is certainly not the man to whom I gave the money.
JOHN DEACON. I am a letter-carrier of the General Post-office; I belong to No. 1 division, which the prisoner belongs to; when the window-clerk has entered in his book the overcharges and rebate, the overcharged letters are brought round to me by one of the sorters, with the money to be allowed on them; this is my signature to the book, acknowledging that I received the overcharges for that day; it is my duty to give to the carriers of the different walks, the overcharged letters and rebate; there is an entry of a letter directed to Mrs. Collins, No. 9, Dorset-street; I returned that letter and the money to the prisoner on Monday, the 12th of August; I gave him 1s. 6d. - he should have given that to Mrs. Collins; the prisoner's signature is on this letter; "Single, J. Borrett;" I cannot swear to his hand-writing, but if it had been written by any other person belonging to his district, I should have given it to the prisoner; seeing that name on the letter, understanding what district it belonged to, and knowing he delivered in that district, I should have given the prisoner the money; I have no doubt of the money and letter being given to him.
Cross-examined. Q. Not the slightest in the world? A. No; I am certain I gave it him; there is nothing particular which calls it to my recollection; I return rebates every morning - I don't remember the identical letter, nor giving him the 1s. 6d. on that identical letter - but I swear, according to the book, that I did return 1s. 6d. on that letter; I will undertake to swear, that I did give the prisoner 1s. 6d., as the rebate on that letter - I gave it to him, to return to Mr. Collins, not Mrs. Collins; I gave him the money to return to the party to whom the letter was addressed; he should not have given it to anybody else, except to a branch of the family.
MR. SHEPHERD. Q. Would he be justified in returning it to anybody, Mr. Collins authorised to receive it? A. Yes; if his wife received the letter, and paid the postage, he would be justified in returning it to her; I never gave the overcharged letter to a carrier, without giving him the money, as he would be answerable for it - he would not take it without the money.
JAMES AUSTIN. I am inspector of letter carriers. On the 2nd of November, I searched the prisoner and found on him an overcharged letter; this is it; the words" Single, J. Borrett," are in his hand-writing; if it were a double letter the word single would not be correct; I asked him what that letter was when I found it, and he said, that was a neglect of his; I asked him if he had received the postage for it, he said, "Yes;" I meant the return of the overcharge; nothing was said about any demand upon him for it - Mrs. Collins name was not mentioned to him.
Cross-examined. Q. Where was this? A. At the post-office.
BENJAMIN CRITCHETT. I am inspector of letter carriers to the post-office. When a carrier receives any overcharge to be returned, it is his duty to give it to the party the same day as he receives it.
MORRIS VINE. I am clerk to the magistrates of Bow-street. I took the depositions in the case between the Crown and the prisoner, before Mr. Halls the magistrate; these are the depositions - and this is Mr. Halls' signature; on the 6th of November, I remember taking down the prisoner's examination; neither threat nor promise was held out to him to confess, it was a voluntary statement on his part, perfectly; it was taken down from his own lips by myself, and read over to him by myself; he did not sign it; he was not asked to sign it; he was asked if he had anything to say - and said he had, and stated what is written in the deposition; when it was read over to him, nothing more was said to him - I never ask a prisoner to sign his examination, unless it be a confession - it was signed by the magistrate (read.)
The prisoner says, "I recollect taking a letter to Mrs. Collins which is produced, and I should imagine I received the rebate, but I have not paid back to Mrs. Collins the rebate, if I had I should have given up the letter. My not delivering the rebate and letter was a neglect of mine, for I forgot to deliver it from day to day."
JAMES LEONARD. I live at No. 14, York-court, East-street, Manchester-square; I have seen the prisoner there delivering letters - he brought me this letter(looking at it) - I paid 2s. 10d. for the postage - I opened it in his presence, and shewed him that it was a single letter; it came from Ireland - I required half the postage back again - he told me to give him the letter in a day or two and he would bring it to me with half the postage back again - I gave him the letter on the Saturday evening following, in High-street, while he was ringing his bell - the letter was directed to East-street, Mary-le-bone - he found me out - I saw him on the Tuesday following in High-street, and spoke to him about returning the letter, but I did not expect he would have it then; I told him I would meet him in High-street, the next evening, while he was ringing his bell, and I met him on the following evening (Thursday) and asked him for my letter; he put his hand to his side, and said he had forgotten it; I never saw him afterwards - I have never received my letter or money back - the letter was first delivered to me on a Tuesday or Wednesday - this Thursday, was the Thursday week afterwards - I had not authorised him to keep the money he was to receive for me, or to dispose of it.
Cross-examined. Q. How many times did you ask him to return you the overcharge? A. Once, I believe; I will swear I asked him once; I asked him for the letter twice; I did not ask him for the money, but, of course, I meant to have the money with the letter - when he clapped his hand to his side, he appeared to be feeling for the letter in his pocket, and then said, he had forgotten it; the letter is now torn across.
MR. ADOLPHUS. Q. It has been out of your possession all this while? A. Yes; when he took the letter from me, he said, he would return me half the postage, which would be 1s. 5d. - when I asked him for the letter I expected the postage also - I know this to be the same letter from the inside of it.
THOMAS BARROWS. I am clerk in the General Postoffice - it is my duty to make rebates on overcharged letters (looking at the letter) - I have no recollection of this letter; but here is a figure of mine on it, which enables me to say that it has passed through my hands(looking at the Rebate book) - here is an entry, dated 28th of October, which I believe refers to this letter; it does not correspond entirely with it; the entry is, "Mr. Wells, 14, York-street, postage 2s. 10d., 1s. 5d. allowed;" sometimes mistakes occur in entering the directions of letters, but not often; I have no doubt whatever but that entry refers to that letter - the book is signed by" J. Deacon."
Cross-examined. Q. Have you any recollection of returning the rebate? A. Not the slightest; these transactions are too numerous for me to recollect them; this 1s. 5d. was put on the letter by me.
Q. Do you swear that when you entered "Mr. Wells, 14, York-street," you were copying from a letter, with"Mr. James Leonard, 14, York-court on it?" A. Yes; I believe it is the same; the direction was obliterated, and I read it "Wells" to the best of my recollection - I misread "Wells" for "James," but I believe it to be the letter from the correspondence between the entry and the letter - I have no recollection of it, except from the book; I read "James" for "Wells;" I did not see the surname - I may have copied that entry from some other letter; to the best of my belief, I did.
MR. SCARLETT. Q. The two figures on the letters are yours? A. I have no doubt of it; I have made no similar entry of 1s. 5d. for any other letter that day - I entered two pages of overcharged letters that morning - when I have a great many it sometimes occurs that one name is put for another - my sight is not very good.
JOHN DEACON. I am letter-carrier of the same division as the prisoner; my signature is to this book - on the 28th of October, here is a letter entered in the name of "Wells, 14, York-street;" I have signed the book for the amount of money for that division, I delivered the letters to the different carriers - the entry here is"Wells, 14, York-street," but I only sign for the amount I receive - I did not receive a letter that day directed to "14, York-street" - there is a letter entered here, "14, York-street," but I did not receive such a letter - my signature is to the book as having received 5s. 9d. for rebates belonging to my division - there is a York-street in our division - this letter is directed "York-court, East-Street, Manchester-square;" that is in the prisoner's delivery - I have no means of saying whether I received that letter with the overcharge - the prisoner receives the rebate letters from me - the direction of this letter is in his walk, and his name is attached to the letter in his writing - the letter shows it has been overcharged, and the money returned - that letter and the rebate would be delivered to him in due course; I should have delivered it to the prisoner - the Post-office date on it is the 23rd of October, when it went out of the office first - I have no doubt but that I delivered that letter to the prisoner, but at what time I cannot say - the address of the letter is not correctly copied into the book; there is no such an address in it for that day; there is an address of "Wells, &c." with exactly the same amount of postage.
Cross-examined. Q. Will you undertake to swear you gave any person the overcharge on that letter? A. Yes, positively but not to the time - it must have been after the 23rd of October, that I gave it to the prisoner - I have no recollection of giving it to him, no more than in the general way, as I gave him them every morning, as he sits in his seat, and the money also - he sits the third man from me, and I gave him the whole sum - I received the rebate from a man named Reed, a sorter; it came from the window of the Inland-office.
GEORGE LEADBITTER. I am a Bow-street officer. On the 2nd of November, I searched the prisoner, and found on him this letter in a pocket-book, in his coat-pocket - there were more letters of the same kind in his pocketbook - I asked him about this one letter in particular; hesaid, he had neglected to return the postage, and that he had to meet the man at the corner of the street, but he had not seen him; he did not mention the man's name, but I inquired why he had not returned the money to Leonard; he said, he had neglected to do so, and had to meet him as before.
GUILTY of Embezzling the 1s. 6d. - Transported for Fourteen Years.
[trialAccount t18331128-135]
Second London Jury, Before Mr. Recorder.
140. MARCUS BERKMAN was indicted for stealing, on the 11th of November, at St. Botolph, without, Aldgate, 3 necklaces, value 19s.; 12 pairs of spectacles, value 7s.; 60 pairs of ear-rings, value 6l.; 12 brooches, value 1l. 8s.; 17 shirt-pins, value 1l. 10s.; 4 pencil-cases, value 4s.; 18 thimbles, value 10s., and 1 wooden-box, value 15s., the goods of Samuel Jacobs, in the dwelling-house of Meyer Oppenheim .
The prisoner being a foreigner had the evidence interpreted to him.
SAMUEL JACOBS. I am a licensed hawker, and deal in jewellery. I have known the prisoner some time; I saw him last year, and I saw him at Oxford once; I first saw him a twelvemonth ago - I went to live at No. 10, Ebenezer-square, in the city, a month ago next Friday; the prisoner came there on the following day; he slept in the same room with me - I had a box containing valuable articles; I delivered it to the landlady to keep for me; the landlady slept in the same room as the box was in - I went to the synagogue on the following Monday; I was going into the country afterwards - I missed my box at eight o'clock on the 11th of November; it contained the property stated in the indictment, which was worth 20l. altogether; the seals were worth 2l.; the ear-rings were worth 5l. 10s.; I am sure they were worth more than 5l. - I have since seen the box; this is it (looking at it) - I have got back all the property except what the pawnbroker has, which is one necklace.
MEYER OPPENHEIM. I live at No. 10, Ebenezer-square, I don't know what parish it is in. Jacobs lodged in my house every time he came to London, and did so in November last; the prisoner also lodged in the same room at the same time - Jacobs delivered his box of trinkets to me to keep; I saw it last on Sunday night; I don't know the day of the month - I went to the synagogue at seven o'clock on Monday morning; the prisoner was then in the same room as I was; I left him there - Jacobs asked me for his box at eight o'clock on Monday morning and it was gone, and the prisoner had left the house; he never returned to the house of his own accord; he owed me 2s.
ANN ROSE. I live with Mrs. Mullet, in Leman-street. I saw the prisoner on Monday, the 11th of November, at Mrs. Mullet's, between ten and eleven o'clock in the morning; he asked if Mrs. Mullet had a room to let; he spoke in English; I thoroughly understood him; Mrs. Mullet appeared to understand him; she spoke to him in English - she said she had a room, but it was empty, and she did not think it would suit him, but she could not accommodate him with a bed - he said then he should like to see the room, and he would go out in the afternoon and buy a bed - he had this box with him when he came; he said he had lost the key of his box and could not open it; he asked Mrs. Mullet if she could give him anything that could open the box - Mrs. Mullet said no, she had nothing; and she said then she would lend him a long nail; he then went up-stairs into the room - I went up immediately after and saw the box open in the room - he asked me if I would pledge four rows of coral beads for him, because he had no money about him; I did so; I pawned them at Mr. Sowerby's, in Cable-street, for 5s.; I gave the money to the prisoner - I did not see him open the box; the prisoner afterwards gave me a pair of ear-rings; I gave them up to the officer afterwards - I saw several articles inside the box, I could not see what they were; I saw ear-rings, beads, spectacles, thimbles, and a good many articles - there were a good many ear-rings, I could not see what number; I saw some shirt-pins, pencil-cases, combs, and a black necklace - the prisoner was taken in custody on Monday night, between ten and eleven o'clock.
WILLIAM SOWERBY. I live at No. 81, Cable-street. I received a hank of beads in pledge from Ann Rose, for 5s.; I have them here - it was on Monday, the 11th of November; I knew her before, as dealing at the shop.
CHARLES PALMER. (policeman H 83). I apprehended the prisoner in a second-floor front-room, in Leman-street, between ten and eleven o'clock on the 11th of November - he asked me what I wanted before he opened the door; I told him my business was respecting a box of jewellery; he replied that he knew nothing about it - I found the box in the same room; this is it, and it contained the articles which are now in it - I searched him and found two pairs of coral earrings, the duplicate of the coral necklace, and 2s., on him; and as I was bringing him out of the house, the prosecutor came in and claimed the articles; Rose came up and gave me the ear-rings - the box had been broken open; I asked if he had the key; he said he had not got it - I know Ebenezer-square, it is in the parish of St. Botolph, without, Aldgate.(Property produced and sworn to).
GUILTY. Aged 20. - Transported for Life.
[trialAccount t18331128-136]
NEW COURT. Tuesday, December 3, 1833.

141. CHRISTIAN DALLINGER was indicted for stealing, on the 16th of November, 1 pair of shoes, value 11s., the goods of William Gilley, to which he pleaded
GUILTY. - Transported for Seven Years.
