142. HENRY ROCKALL and HENRY BROWN were indicted for stealing, on the 27th of October, 1 handkerchief, value 3s., the goods of Edward Roberts.
ISAAC ISAACS (police-sergeant E 8). On Sunday, the 27th of October, I was in Holborn at a quarter-pasteight o'clock. I saw the two prisoners close behind the prosecutor; Rockall stepped up in front of the other prisoner, and took a handkerchief from the prosecutor's pocket - I took hold of him immediately; he scuffled and threw the handkerchief down - I told my brother officer to go and tell the prosecutor that his pocket was picked- the prosecutor returned and took up his handkerchief - we took the prisoner to the station-house - Brown stood close by; he was taken by another officer - I had not seen the prisoners speaking to one another.
Cross-examined by MR. PHILLIPS. Q. Did the other officer see the handkerchief thrown down? A. I do not know; he was close by me - he was running after the prosecutor when it was thrown down.
EDMUND DAVIS (police-constable E 86). I was there, and saw the prisoners walking by the side of each other; I did not see them speak together - I saw Rockall step before the other prisoner, behind the gentleman and lady; he put his hand into the gentleman's pocket, and drew the handkerchief out - I ran after the gentleman, and told him; he missed his handkerchief - we came back - Isaacs had hold of Rockall, and Brown was standing by; he might have made his escape - I had seen the prisoners before.
EDWARD ROBERTS. I lodge in Regent-street. On the 27th of October, I was walking in Holborn; the officer told me my handkerchief was gone - I returned and picked it up within a few yards of the prisoners.
Rockall's Defence (written). About eight o'clock on the evening in question, I was hastening home, through Holborn, to my father's house, when to my surprise, I was taken into custody on the present charge; and what added to my surprise was, that the prisoner Brown was taken as an accomplice. I can assure you, my Lord, that I never saw him, before he was apprehended.
Benjamin Wyatt and William Stayer gave Rockall a good character.
ROCKALL - GUILTY. Aged 19. - Transported for Seven Years.
BROWN - NOT GUILTY
[trialAccount t18331128-138]
143. RICHARD KING was indicted for embezzlement.
THOMAS WOODWARD. I am a furniture-broker, and live in Carnaby-street. The prisoner was my porter for fourteen or fifteen years; he was occasionally authorised to receive money for me, which it was his duty to account for immediately he returned - on the 26th of August, I packed up two mahogany night-tables, and directed him to take them to No. 15, Bruton-street - the bill I gave him was 2l. 8s., but I believe there was a mistake, and the lady paid him 2l. 7s. - he ought to have returned to me immediately, where I had other work for him to do; but I saw no more of him till the officer took him on the 22nd of October.
ELIZABETH SMITH. I am housekeeper to John Woolmer, Esq., of Bruton-street. On the 26th of August, the prisoner brought the two tables to me, and I paid him 2l. 7s. - this is the bill.
The prisoner put in a written defence, stating, that he had gone into a public-house and fallen asleep, during which time the money was taken from his pocket. - Peter John Dorrington gave him a good character, and engaged to employ him.
GUILTY. Aged 36. - Confined Two Days.
[trialAccount t18331128-139]
144. EDWARD JONES was indicted for stealing, on the 19th of November, 1 child's waggon, value 15s., the goods of James Vine.
JOHN HIGGINS. I saw the prisoner on the 19th of November, with a child's waggon; he asked a person at the corner of the street, if she would buy it - he then asked me; I asked him the price; he said 8s. - he stood talking a few minutes, and then said I should have it for 1s. - he said he would not sell it so cheap, but Smithfield-market was not open; and he told me to buy it soon, as he wanted to go back to steal some brooms - he then went on, and I followed him; he went into Mr. Swale's shop, and came out, and he said he had sold it for 5s. - he told me he had sold three dozen brass cocks, at Elme's, in Playhouse-yard, for 1s., and they had told him if he did not take that they would send for the policeman, and have him taken - I went there, and while I was there three boys came running in with a dressed ham, which they sold for 2s., and they were to have one pound of it for their supper - the policeman came, and had the ham.
Prisoner. I did not say any thing to him. Witness. Yes, he did; and he asked me to go with him, and steal a till, and said he would give me half the money - he told me the shop he had stolen the waggon from, but when the prosecutor came, he did not say a word.
WILLIAM SWALE. I keep a shop in Coppice-row, Clerkenwell. I was sitting in my shop when the prisoner came with this waggon - he asked if I wanted to buy it - I said "No;" he said it was a very good one - I asked what he wanted for it - he said 6s.; I said, "I bought one, and it broke down" - he said this would not do so; that his father had made it, who lived at No. 24, Willow-gardens, Hoxton - I asked him if his father repaired such things; he said, "Yes," and I told him to call any time, and he should have the other to repair; he said his father would be glad of the job - I bought this one of him.
JAMES VINE. I keep a toy-shop, in Hedge-row, Islington. This is my waggon - I lost it on the 19th of November.
GUILTY. Aged 12. - Transported for Seven Years.
[trialAccount t18331128-140]
145. ANDREW GARDINER was indicted for stealing, on the 19th of November, 2 cruet-stands, value 6s.; 7 bottles, value 8s., and 1 tea-spoon, value 1s. 6d., the goods of Thomas Wood.
HENRY ROBERTS. I am waiter to Mr. Thomas Wood, at Wood's-hotel, Arundel-street. On the 19th of November, I left two cruet-stands on a shelf in the coffee-room, at eleven o'clock at night, when I went down to supper - I left no one in the room; I went up again in three minutes, and saw the prisoner with the two cruet-stands in his hand - he was coming out at the door as I went in; he then placed them on the table and walked out - I do not know whether he had taken them up to look at, or whether he was going to take them away - theshelf they had been on is about a yard and a half from the door - he was going out; I caught hold of him - he got from me, and broke the window and ran out.
Cross-examined by MR. DOANE. Q. Do you know that he went there to measure a gentleman for some clothes? A. No; he had these stands in his hand - he was not disguising them - I do not know what he meant to do with them; he ran when he had broken the window - he gave his address, but I forget it.
WILLIAM ROBERTS (police-constable C 152). I heard the alarm, and saw the prisoner running; I stopped him and took him back - he seemed very much confused - he said a gentleman appointed to meet him there to pay him a bill.
The prisoner stated in his defence, that he had gone there to meet a gentleman on business; and no one being there, he had taken the stands up to look at.
NOT GUILTY.
[trialAccount t18331128-141]
146. ELIZA WILLIAMS alias CONNER was indicted for, that she having been convicted of feloniously uttering a counterfeit sixpence, and a counterfeit shilling, afterwards, to wit, on the 1st of November, feloniously, unlawfully, and wilfully, did utter and dispose of, to Joseph Hall, a counterfeit sixpence, well knowing it to be a counterfeit; against the statute, &c.
MESSRS. SCARLETT and GURNEY conducted the prosecution.
CALEB EDWARD POWELL. I produce the record of the conviction, of Eliza Williams, at the Middlesex Session, December, 1832. (read.)
JOHN SUMMERSELL. I am turnkey at the House of Correction - the prisoner was tried at the December Session, 1832 - she was six months in the House of Correction.
JOSEPH COLLIS. I live at No. 100, Great Saffron-hill. On the 29th of October, I saw the prisoner in Barbican - I watched her into a pastry-cook's shop, and into a public-house, and then to Mr. Wright's, in Aldersgate-street - I went in there and Mr. Wright showed me a bad 6d. - I saw the prisoner next in Hatton-garden office, on the 2nd of November.
Prisoner. I never saw that man till I was at the office- he then asked me if I did not go into a pastry-cook's shop, which I am certain I never did; I was over the water the whole of that day - he has sworn entirely false. Witness. I am certain it was her; I had seen her before; it was between four and five o'clock.
JAMES WRIGHT. I keep the King's Arms, in Aldersgate street. On the 29th of October, the prisoner came to my house, and called for some gin, she put down a bad 6d. - I said "It is a very bad one, did you not know it?" she said, "No;" I said, "I shall detain it," she did not scruple at that, but walked out without drinking the gin - I showed the same 6d. to Collis, who came in at the door at that time - I nailed it to a post, in my bar till the officer came - I then gave it to him.
Prisoner. I was not in his shop; I was over the water, at work, from morning till evening. Witness. I am quite sure of her person - it was between four and five o'clock.
JOSEPH HALL. I keep a public-house, in Gloucester-street, Queen-square . On the 1st of November, the prisoner came to my house, she had two pennyworths of gin, and offered a bad 6d. - I told her it was bad; she said, she did not know it; I held it over the candle and melted half of it away - she went out, and I followed her into Holborn, and saw her go into the King's-head; I went in with a police-officer, and saw the prisoner put down a sixpence on the counter; she saw us, and put something in her mouth; the officer took it from her; it was a bad sixpence; I marked it and gave it to the officer.
JOSEPH CLEMENTS (police-constable E 102). I was on duty, and was called by the last witness - we watched the prisoner into the King's-head, in Holborn; what he has stated is correct - I saw her put the sixpence on the counter; the landlord pushed it back to her, she put it into her mouth; I seized her mouth, she dropped it, and the witness took it up - I received this part of a 6d. from him, and this one from Mr. Wright.
JOHN FIELD. I am inspector of counterfeit coin, to the Mint - these two sixpences and this part of one, are all counterfeit.
GUILTY. Aged 22. - Transported for Seven Years.
[trialAccount t18331128-142]
147. JOHN HOWSE alias WASHBOURNE was inindicted for, that he having been convicted of unlawfully uttering a counterfeit shilling, and at the same time having another counterfeit shilling in his possession; and afterwards uttering another counterfeit shilling; he afterwards on the 27th of October, unlawfully did utter to Elizabeth Pollard, a counterfeit half-crown; well knowing it to be counterfeit.
MESSRS. SCARLETT and GURNEY conducted the prosecution.
CALEB EDWARD POWELL. I produce a record of the prisoner's former conviction. (read.)
JOHN SUMMERSELL. I am turnkey at the House of Correction - I know the prisoner; he was convicted at the July Session, 1832, and was twelve months in the House of Correction.
JOSHUA BROWN. I am a butcher, and live in Gravel-lane, Southwark. On the 17th of October, the prisoner came to my shop, for a bit of meat, which came to 6 1/2d. he said, "I am a poor man, let me have it for 6d." - I said, he might take it; and he threw me down half-a-crown, which was a bad one - I asked him where he got it - he said, from his boy, who went out sweeping - I asked where he lived, but he would not tell me - he said, he had seven more of them on the Saturday before - I told him they must be bad ones - he said, they were not - I gave him in charge - I gave him back the half-crown- but I took it from him again; the policeman had it last.
Prisoner. You went to get the change, and you said, you had taken a bad one on the Saturday night before; and it was as likely to be that one, as it was mine - you did not say mine was had before. Witness. Yes; I did.
JOHN HAMILTON (police-sergeant M 10). I took the prisoner and have the half-crown which I received from this witness - the prisoner was discharged.
GEORGE HUGGINS. I am a news-agent, and live inthe Broadway, Westminster. On the 27th of October, the prisoner came and looked at some publications, he selected a Saturday Magazine, which came to a penny - he laid down this shilling - I said, "Have you any more of this sort?" - he asked, what I meant - I said, I was perfectly satisfied, he knew what I meant, and I went round and forced him out of the house, as it was Sunday morning, and I was busy - I am quite sure the prisoner is the man, he was dressed as a sweep.
ELIZABETH POLLARD. I am the wife of William Pollard - he is a newsman and an optician, in Rochester-row, Westminster. On Sunday, the 27th of October, at half-past six o'clock in the evening, the prisoner came to our house, and asked for a newspaper to read - I told him we did not lend papers to read, unless we had a deposit left; he laid down half-a-crown - I said, "I don't think this is a good one" - he said, "I wish I a had score of them" - I held the 2s. in my hand while I felt the half-crown - he took the 2s. very quickly out of my hand, and was going out, he dropped 1s., and asked for the light to get it; my boy went with the light, but the prisoner found the 1s. and ran off - my brother pursued and caught him.
Prisoner. Did you not lay the 2s. on the table. Witness. No; you took them out of my hand, and went out as quick as you could; you dropped 1s. and picked it up by the light of the moon.
GEORGE GLOVER. I am brother of this witness - I saw the prisoner come in - I received this half-crown from my sister - I followed the prisoner and caught him four or five hundred yards off, running as fast as he could - I gave him, and the half-crown to the officer.
HENRY JAMES PITT (police-sergeant B 13). I took the prisoner, and have the half-crown.
JOHN FIELD. These two half-crowns are counterfeits.
Prisoner She did not mark the half-crown I gave her till it had been out of her possession; and when I came back, she said, she believed it was that; if it was bad why did she give me the change, which she laid on the counter.
GUILTY. Aged 29. - Transported for Seven Years.
[trialAccount t18331128-143]
148. JOHN BURKE was indicted for stealing, on the 8th of November, 1 tripod, value 5l., the goods of Henry Nurse.
HENRY NURSE. I am a carver and gilder, and live in Old Cavendish-street. This is my tripod - I did not miss it, till I was told it was taken on the 8th of November - I got some bills printed, and at three o'clock in the afternoon I saw it at Mr. Aldous - it is worth 5l., and had been on the step of my door - I had not seen it that day, but I saw it when we closed the shop the evening before.
JAMES ALDOUS. I am a pawnbroker, and live in Berwick-street, Soho. I have known the prisoner a long time - in the forenoon of the 8th of November, he brought this tripod to my house - I asked him who he brought it from; he said, from Mr. Newcombe, a respectable gentleman, and whom I have known some years; from whom he had brought things before - I asked him if he was quite sure; he said, "Yes," and he wanted 2l. on it, which I let him have; in the course of the day some bills were brought describing it as stolen, and I gave notice of it.
EDWARD DAVIS (police-constable E 86). I apprehended the prisoner - I told him he was charged with stealing a tripod - I took him to the station-house, and he said, the next morning, that he had pawned it at Mr. Aldous for 2l.
Prisoner's Defence (written). I am a porter, on the day mentioned in the indictment, I was at the corner of Rathbone-place, and was accosted by a man decently attired, who asked me to carry a parcel into Berwick-street, he went with me, when we arrived at the house of Mr. Aldons, the pawnbroker, he desired me to go in, and asked whether they would take it in as a pledge, saying, that it was for Mr. Newcombe. I went in, and asked the question, and was told that it would be taken in. I then went out, took the article from Mr. Newcombe, and pledged it for two sovereigns, I gave the duplicate, and the money to Mr. Newcombe, and receive 2s. for my work. I went away, and went to my brother's in Whitechapel, and received 19s. from my brother, for my mother (which he can prove); to pay a part of the funeral expenses of my father.
ANN TUFT. I live opposite the prosecutor - I saw a man come to take this stand away, between eight and nine o'clock; I cannot swear to the man, but he was a short man similar to the prisoner.
GUILTY. Aged 17. - Transported for Seven Years.
[trialAccount t18331128-144]
149. JOHN AXHAM was indicted for stealing, on the 10th of November, 1 watch, value 30s.; and 1 coat, value 2s., the goods of Joseph Axham.
HANNAH AXHAM. I am mother-in-law to the prisoner, I have no children of my own - my husband is a smith, and lives in Phillip's-buildings, St. Pancras. On the 9th of November, my husband's watch was on the table in the bed-room, and on the Tuesday following I missed it - this is the watch.
WILLIAM JOHN BIRD. I am shopman to a pawnbroker. I have the watch, which I took in of an older lad than the prisoner for 10s.; in the name of Edward Hobbs, No. 10, Seymour-street.
EDMUND COGNEY (police-constable S 169). I took the prisoner in the street - I asked what he had done with his father's coat and watch - he said, he and another lad had pawned the watch in Holborn; and the coat he had sold to a Jew in the street for 3d. - I found the watch - I did not tell him it would be better for him to confess.
JOSEPH AXHAM. What my wife has stated is true; this is my watch, but my coat is lost - the prisoner was absent a week, and then came and took the coat while I was out.
GUILTY. Aged 13. - Transported for Seven Years.
[trialAccount t18331128-145]
150. THOMAS DUXBERRY was indicted for stealing on the 17th of November, 1 watch, value 4l.; 2 seals, value 30s.; 1 chain, value 1s.; and 1 hat, value 1s. 6d. the goods of William Wilks, from his person; and WILLIAM CHRISTOPHER CURALL for receiving the same, well knowing them to have been stolen.
WILLIAM WILKS. I am a coach wheelwright. On Sunday morning the 17th of November, I was going home in a cab - I had had some liquor with a friend, but was sober; I got out of the cab between one and two o'clock in themorning, in the Edgware-road - as I was getting out of the cab I felt something touch my waistcoat - I put my hand to it, the cab turned off at that moment, and threw me off the step on my head - I had paid the fare, and the cab drove off down Chapel-street; the prisoner Duxberry and another person came up to me, and asked where I was going - I said, to No. 5, Market-street, and Duxberry said, he would assist me home - when we got to my lodging- I knocked two knocks at the door, my landlady came to the window, and said, "Who is there" - I said, "The wheeler" - she said, "I will call Bill" - meaning another lodger - she called him, and she came to the window again, and said, "Bill is coming" - the prisoner Duxberry then snatched my hat off my head, and ran away - the hat was worth 1s. 6d., and there was a handkerchief in it which fell out on the pavement - I then discovered that my watch was gone; but whether he took it or not I cannot say - it might have been the cab-man - I had two seals and a key to my watch - one seal and key were warranted to me to be plated on silver - a person named Potts, a friend of mine had been in the cab with me - I know nothing of the other prisoner.
Cross-examined by MR. HODGES. Q. Where had you been drinking that night? A. I had been having some half and half; and then we went the Union, and had a quartern of rum between three of us - I was not the worse for liquor - I fell on my head from the cab, and that stunned me - I fell from the step, because the cab turned away before I had time to step off the step - I was not certain but what the cab-man took the watch, for he had been sitting on the seat with me, and my friend drove the cab; I had some bills printed, saying, that my watch was taken by the cab-man, as I suspected him - I was not certain who took it, but I know Duxberry took my hat - he and a man named Grace picked me up - my friend drove off in the cab- he did not know of my being thrown off - the first words that I can recollect that Duxberry said, was "Where are you going" - I told him, and he said, he would assist me home I walked home quietly - we had no fun in going along - my landlady Mrs. Smith, looked out of the window, and said, "Who is there" - I said, "The wheeler," - Duxberry then took my hat - I did not call to him to stop - I picked up my handkerchief, and staid at the door till my fellow lodger came.
JAMES TURNER. I am a labourer. I lodge at Mr. Baker's at Kilburn; I was in Bell-street, Paddington, on Sunday morning, the 17th of November, about nine o'clock; there were several persons there and I saw the prisoner Duxberry; he asked me if I had seen William Curall; I said, I had not; he put his hand into his right trowsers pocket, pulled out a watch and said, he had picked a man up who had tumbled out of a cab lumpy, and he had got that and his tile - lumpy means tipsy, and tile means a hat - he then put the watch into his pocket again - it was a silver watch with a chased case; not a plain case; it had two seals, a key, and a steel chain; I am sure of that - while we were in conversation, William Curall came up, and Duxberry told me to stand on one side as he wanted to speak to him - I stood about a couple of yards from them, and saw Duxberry take the watch out of his pocket and shew it to Curall; Curall then took it into his hands, and they were in conversation; Curall then gave Duxberry two half-crowns, and put the watch into his own pocket, and went away; Duxberry then asked me if I would have a glass of rum; I said yes, and he took me to the Green-man, and we had a quartern, and he paid for it out of one of the half-crowns; we walked on as far as the Coach and Horses, and then he asked me to go home and have a bit of breakfast with him, and I went with him to Grace's where he lodged; I had some bread and meat and a cup of tea; I then left him - I went there again at dinner time; I had some bread and meat and then came away; I left him there and did not see him again till Monday evening, about nine o'clock; he was very tipsy and had a cap on - I saw him two or three times, but did not have any conversation with him till the Friday following, when I saw him in the Edgware-road; he said, he wanted to see William Curall as he had promised him 1s.; he said, he had fooled away the watch very foolishly; I told him there was a paper in a window and I took him and shewed it him; he said, "Yes, that was the watch and this is the tile I have on, and I had better do away with it;" I did not give information of this.
Cross-examined by MR. CLARKSON. Q. Now as Duxberry told you he had found a man lumpy and he had got that and his tile, how soon was it after that you gave information of this? A. I did not know of anything being lost till I saw the bill; I believe there was one sovereign reward on the bill; I do not know whether I expect to get it - I do not know whether I hope to get it - I came here because the prosecutor is a poor man, and I thought he should not lose his hat - I went before the magistrate and Curall was admitted to bail - I have not seen the watch since - I did not know who had lost these things, till I saw the bill on the Friday following, and then I went and told that a man I had seen in the street, told me he had stolen a watch and a hat - I live at Mr. Baker's opposite the Red Lion, at Kilburn.
Q. Were you ever known to Mr. Baker till this time? A. The carman knew me, but Mr. Baker did not; I lodge in the buildings where he keeps hay and that, as I was out of employ; I did not lodge in the open air; I lodged in Mr. Baker's hay-loft for three weeks - I never asked his permission; he did not know it - I am not known at the Delaware Arms - I was never lurking about there; I have been in there and had a pint of beer, but I was never lurking about there.
Q. Then if I were to produce anybody who had seen you lurking about there you would be surprised? A. No; I should not be surprised at all - I know Matthew Caylor Smith.
Q. Do you remember saying to him that you were not going to walk about without shoes, and to starve, as you knew where there were some fowls that would do very well? A. No, sir; I never said so nor anything of the kind - I was not without shoes - I knew there were fowls where I lodged - when I told his lordship and the jury that I lodged at Mr. Baker's I did not mean to represent that I lodged in his house - I was neverin any trouble myself; I was never acqainted with crime in any way - I never did any man an injury, nor any fowls - I knew that tile meant a hat and that lumpy meant tipsy, because I had heard men say so in the street many times - he did not say he had picked a man up who was lumpy and had got his ticker - I know that ticker means a watch - I have heard that too.
Cross-examined by MR. HODGES. Q. Now I want you to tell me something about the robbery at Barnet? A. I was robbed at Barnet, at ten o'clock at night; I gave evidence that Samuel Sturm had robbed me - I do not know what the sentence on him was - I am sure I was not taken up - I gave evidence that I was robbed of my knife, tobacco box, 9s. 6d. and five duplicates, out of my pocket - I charged three men with it, but two of them were not taken till after the assizes - I never was a witness where two men were convicted; I will swear that - I get my living by my labour at anything I can get to do - I have been employed for months together in the Regents Park and other places - I have known Duxberry for years - he has worked with me - I did not tell him he had done wrong when he gave me this information, nor when I breakfasted with him - I had seen Curall a few times before.
MARY GRACE. The prisoner Duxberry lodged at my house; on Saturday night, the 16th of November, I went to bed about eleven o'clock; he was not at home then; he lived with a woman in that house; I saw him at breakfast-time the next morning - Turner was there, but I did not know his name then; they were both in liquor, but the witness Turner was so drunk, that he was not able to sit at the table, he sat on the foot of my bed; Duxberry went out soon after breakfast, and when he returned home, he went to bed; I did not notice any hat in my room in particular, but I know Duxberry had a hat, and a cap, when he came to my place.
Q. Recollect what you have sworn, did you take no notice of any hat? A. There was a hat there, but I did not take notice of it; Duxberry left me, and a day or two afterwards, the woman he lived with, came and asked me for Tom's old hat, and I gave it her.
SAMUEL PHILLIPS (police-constable T 54). I went to Grace's shop about four o'clock on the Friday - I searched the privy, and found these pieces of hat down there; the prosecutor was with me.
WILLIAM WILKS. This is part of the hat I lost - here is a part of the crown which I can swear to, for it was standing on my tool chest one day, and I dropped an inch and a half chisel on it, which cut it through; there was another piece I could have sworn to, but it was very filthy and we did not bring it.
Witness for the defence.
MATTHEW CAYLOR SMITH. I am a labouring man, but my mother supports me when I am at home; she is a laundress, and lives in Bury-place, Bell-street, Edgware-road; I have seen Turner about - he told me he was not going to walk about without shoes and to starve; he was told to look for work, and he said if he went walking about, he should not have shoes to go to work with; he said he had not slept in a bed for a month, but down at Kilburn some nights in a hay-rick, and sometimes in a shed, and sometimes in a building; he said there were bantum fowls there, and he intended to have shoes before long, or something to get shoes with.
BAKER. I am a farmer, and live at Kilburn; I am afraid Turner lodged about my place to my inconvenience - I lost a great many fowls; when my hay is put out for my cattle, Turner walks in and takes the hay and covers himself with it; there was a very bad character lodged with him.
JOHN GARRETT. I lodge at No.'9, Bond-street, in the next room to William Curall; on the 17th of November, I was at home; I heard him in his room at nine, at half-past nine, and at ten o'clock; at half-past ten o'clock I was in his room - he was then at breakfast, and had no coat on; I am confident he had not been out that morning.
GEORGE METCALF. The prisoner Curall lodged in my house, in the next room to the last witness; on the 17th of November, I left my house at half-past ten o'clock or rather better; he was at home then, and I am positive he had not been out; I answered for his appearance before the magistrate; he had lodged a year and ten months with me.
JAMES GRACE. The prisoner Duxberry lodged with me; on Saturday night, the 16th of November, he came home - I think it was not after twelve o'clock; I was standing at the corner of Lisson-street, and the pork butcher's shop was not shut up; I got the mattress out, and made a bed up for him in the same room I slept in, but he came home after and went to bed - he had been drinking a little.
WILLIAM WILKS. This is the man who was with Duxberry when he picked me up.
MARY SMITH. The prosecutor lodged at my house; he came home and knocked twice at my door - I looked out and saw him, and Duxberry was with him, he had a blue cap on.
Cross-examined by MR. HODGES. Q. What time was this? A. The clock struck two as I was putting the window up - I can swear that Duxberry was the person I saw; he had a blue coat and a blue cap on; I picked him out of the cell at High-street.
DUXBERRY - GUILTY. Aged 24.
Transported for Seven Years.
CURALL - NOT GUILTY.
[trialAccount t18331128-146]
151. LOUISA MANTZ was indicted for stealing, on the 18th of November, 1 half-sovereign, the money of James Feltham, from his person.
JAMES FELTHAM. I live in Old Ford-lane, Bethnal-green, and am a weaver. On the 18th of November, I was in Shoreditch at half-past nine o'clock; I met with the prisoner as I was loitering round some ballad-singers; there was a crowd there - I had a half-sovereign in my left hand pocket, wrapped up in a piece of white paper; I had put it there about six o'clock in the evening- I did not stop more than five minutes with the balladsingers; I was outside the crowd, and when I went away the prisoner and another woman came after me, and accosted me; we walked on talking about the ballads, and I turned up Bird-cage-walk, which was my nearestway home - the prisoner asked me where I was going; I said down that road, and through the fields. had she any inclination to walk with me; she did, and we went down Bird-cage-walk: I felt something in my pocket, but I had no suspicion she was robbing me till she suddenly turned back; I then felt my pocket and it was turned inside out - I pursued the prisoner, but I first passed the other woman, who the prisoner said was her sister - I came up with the prisoner who was walking very fast; I said I would give her in charge of the police, unless she would return my money: I kept on with her till I found the officer, and gave her in charge - she wished me to go back to her friend, and said they would see what they could do; I said it was too late.
Cross-examined by MR. CLARKSON. Q. Did you tell the magistrate that the prisoner wished you to go back to her friend? A. Yes - I did not ask her to go with me; but she was going the same road, and I went across the fields in my road home - when I missed my half-sovereign, I told her if she would give me my half-sovereign, I would say no more about it - I had not been at work that day; I had been to Blackfriars - the prisoner was alone with me when I missed my money, but the woman she said was her sister was a few yards from us; the prisoner took off her pattens before I came up to her, and after she left me she went up to the other woman and gave her her pattens - it was dark; when we walked down the Bird-cage-walk; I felt something at my pocket which I did not take notice of; I had my umbrella under my arm, and the hand was withdrawn in a moment from my pocket - I had not time to put my hand to it - I don't know what she did with the half-sovereign unless she swallowed it - I had had a little rum and shrub with them before I went into the Bird-cage-walk; I did not ask them to go and have it - they asked me to treat them; I paid for that out of my waistcoat pocket - I kept my hand on the pocket where my half-sovereign was while I was at the ballad-singers; I told her it was a nasty song, and she said she never heard such an one.
COURT. Q. Might not this have dropped out of your pocket when you were standing together? A. We did not stand.
CHARLES GRANT (police-constable H 25). The prisoner was given into my custody; I found nothing on her; her companion came down to the station-house, but nothing was found on her.
Prisoner's Defence. This man came up to me and my sister as we were going along, he asked us to have some rum and shrub, and then he asked me to go across the fields with him, which I refused; we went down Bird-cage-walk to get from him, but he followed me; he wanted me to go down a court, which I refused; he then said I had robbed him.
NOT GUILTY.
[trialAccount t18331128-147]
153. WILLIAM PHILLIPS and WILLIAM HATTON were indicted for stealing, on the 2nd of November, 1 coat, value 18s., the goods of John Wright. Hatton pleaded.
GUILTY. Aged 18. - Transported for Seven Years.
JOHN WYLES. I live with Mr. Smith, of Rufford's, buildings, Islington. On the 2nd of November I saw Hatton minding a cart and horse, and Phillips stood against a wall; I saw Phillips go up to the cart, take a coat out, and run down George-yard, and Hatton after him: I went up to the cart and waited till the prosecutor came; I told him of it.
JOHN WRIGHT. I live in Bowling-green-lane. I left my cart and horse in care of Hatton - when I returned my coat was gone; Wyles told me of it - I went down George-yard, but could not see the prisoner; Wyles afterwards pointed Phillips out and he was taken.
Phillips. Wyles has been tried himself.
JOHN WYLES. Twelve months ago, I was destitute of a home, I went out thieving, and stole beef and bread.
Hatton. He buys anything; he bought that cap he has in his hand of a boy.
JOHN WYLES. I bought this for 1s. 6d. of a boy, but I don't buy stolen things; I have been in the House of Correction about three times.
COURT. Q. What were you there for? A. Once was for a pair of boots.
Hatton. He was there once for stealing a till with two more boys; one of them was the boy he bought that cap of, and he is in this goal now.
PHILLIPS - NOT GUILTY.
[trialAccount t18331128-148]
152. JAMES FORD was indicted for stealing, on the 19th of October, 1 watch, value 30s.; 1 guard-chain, value 9s., and 1 watch-key, value 3d., the goods of Simon Goodman Jones.
SIMON GOODMAN JONES. I live shopman at No. 155, Tottenham-court-road. On getting up on Saturday, the 19th of October, I left my watch in my bed-room; at seven o'clock in the morning I saw it on the hook where it usually hung; I desired a servant to put it into a drawer, which she did - at four o'clock in the afternoon I went to inquire about it and it was gone; the prisoner was porter in the house; he was taken, and my guard and key, and the duplicate were found on him.
SARAH PULLEN. I brought down the watch, and put it into the drawer, at ten o'clock - I looked for it at four o'clock and it was gone - there was no one but the prisoner in the kitchen when I put it into the drawer.
ALEXANDER BROWN (police-constable E 67). I took the prisoner - I found this guard chain in his pocket, and the key in this bag, on his person - this duplicate was by his side - he said, he had taken the watch.
WILLIAM ALFRED PUTNAM. I am a pawnbroker - the prisoner pawned the watch with me.
Prisoner's Defence. A man named Taplin was in the habit of calling there for the shopmen's linen; and on that day he called for 17s. 6d. due to him, from Mr. Ware, but he found he had been discharged. He then met me at the gateway, and asked me to pawn his watch for him, as he had not received his money - and said, he would give me the guard and key, if I would pawn the watch; and he would see me again in the evening, and receive the money, and the duplicate. I never saw the watch till Taplin gave it to me, and the witness cannot deny that he had been in the kitchen, where she says she had placed the watch.
WILLIAM ALFRED PUTNAM. The watch was pawned about four o'clock in the afternoon, for 1l. 5s. in the name of Taplin.
SARAH PULLEN. There was no such a person there that I know of.
GUILTY. Aged 22. - Confined Eight Months.
[trialAccount t18331128-149]
154. CHARLES HENSON was indicted for stealing, on the 30th of October, 1 watch, value 8s.; 1 seal, value 1s.; 1 watch key, value 1s.; 1 snuff-box, value 2s.; and 1 tea-spoon, value 3s. the goods of Daniel Riley, his master.
DANIEL RILEY. I am a tailor, and live in Bowling-green-lane. The prisoner was in my employ - I left this watch, in a drawer, in the room, in which we worked - it had belonged to my deceased father-in-law - I had seen it about the end of September, and did not miss it till the prisoner was in custody.
THOMAS WELLS. I am a pawnbroker. I produce this watch; the prisoner offered it to pawn, to redeem two articles he had pawned before - I stopped it on suspicion.
WILLIAM BRITTAIN. I am a pawnbroker. I produce a snuff-box, pawned, I believe, by the prisoner, in the name of John Bell.
DANIEL RILEY. This snuff-box is mine.
WILLIAM BARTON (police-sergeant G 1). I took the prisoner.
Prisoner. I took the watch to redeem some other things.
GUILTY. Aged 18. - Confined Three Months.
[trialAccount t18331128-150]
155. ANN KIRKBY was indicted for stealing, on the 21st of October, 1 shift, value 2s.; 1 night-cap, value 1s. 6d.; and 1 handkerchief, value 1s., the goods of Mildred Sutton.
MILDRED SUTTON. I live servant with Mr. Miers, in Chiswell-street. I had a shift, a night-cap, and a handkerchief, in my box; on the 21st of October I missed them - I had left the box locked on the Friday before, and I found it locked, but these things were missing.
Cross-examined by MR. PHILLIPS. Q. Had you ever lent the prisoner any thing to wear? A. No; she never asked me - she never wore anything of mine, to my knowledge - we slept together two nights - she had leave to go out for a holiday on the Saturday evening.
THOMAS SEAL (police-constable C 145). I took the prisoner - the prosecutrix searched her, and found the shift on her; this handkerchief was in her hand; and this night-cap was found at the place where she lived, in Long-alley - I found three keys on her which would open the prosecutrix's boxes.
Cross-examined. Q. Was there not much more valuable property in the prosecutrix's box? A. Yes; both her box and the prisoner's were full.
ISAAC LEVY MIERS. The prisoner lived servant with me, but she had left me, and left her direction where she was gone - she had not taken away her boxes - they are at my house now, full of things.
NOT GUILTY.
[trialAccount t18331128-151]
156. BENJAMIN BALL was indicted for stealing, on the 1st of November, 1 hammer, value 9d. the goods of Isaac Musgrove.
ISAAC MUSGROVE. I am a dustman, and live at No. 6, Bath-row, Dalston - this hammer is mine - I saw it on Saturday evening, the 16th of November, in my yard, when I locked my shed up, and missed it on the Sunday morning - I was afterwards going by a shop, at Kingsland, and saw my hammer - I asked the price of it, and they said, 6d.; I said, it was mine - I had seen the prisoner about my premises, on the afternoon of the 16th of November.
JANE TOLLADY. I bought this hammer of the prisoner on the Saturday evening, for 3d.; that was what he asked me for it, and that I gave him.
ISAAC MUSGROVE. It had been on the wall, inside my shed - the gates were locked, and the person who got in broke through another man's hedge to get in - I know this hammer to be mine.
GUILTY. Aged 18. - Confined Four Months.
[trialAccount t18331128-152]
157. ELEANOR NOWLAND was indicted for stealing, on the 23rd of November, 1 table-cloth, value 1s. 6d.; 1 towel, value 1s.; 1 napkin, value 1s. 6d.; 2 petticoats, value 9s.; 1 pair of drawers, value 3s.; and 1 handkerchief, value 5s. the goods of James Abbott.
2nd COUNT, stating them to be the goods of John Pretty.
SARAH PRETTY. On Saturday week last, the prisoner came to our house, and said, she came for the mangling, from No. 7, Guildford-street - I said, "We have none from there, and you don't live there" - she said, "Yes, I do" - I brought her down a bundle, and said, "Is this yours" - she said, "Yes;" I asked her if she could tell me anything that was in it - she said, "Yes, a silk pocket handkerchief" - I opened it and found there was a silk handkerchief in it; she gave me 1 1/2d. for the mangling, and took the bundle away which contained these things.
Cross-examined by MR. PHILLIPS. Q. Do you know Mrs. Harrod? A. No; I afterwards met the prisoner in the street, and saw her go into a draper's shop - I told her the things were not her's; they belonged to No. 34, Gray's-inn-lane - I took her to our house, and she was taken into custody.
COURT. Q. Did you give her the articles stated in this indictment? A. Yes; there was a table-cloth, a towel, a napkin, and the other articles stated; which we had received from Mr. Abbott, No. 34, Gray's-inn-lane.
CHARLES PEARCE (police-sergeant G 45). The prisoner was given into my custody - I went to No. 1, Crawford-passage, where she lodged - I found these articles in a drawer, in the second-floor front room.
Cross-examined. Q. How did you find that they were her lodgings? A. By knowing a young man who lived in that house - the drawers were not locked - these things were on the top of the other things, and seemed to be fresh mangled.
MARY PRETTY. I am the wife of John Pretty, and the mother of Sarah Pretty - I received these things from Mr. Abbott, No. 34, Gray's-inn-lane.
SARAH PRETTY re-examined. Q. What did she say when you saw her the second time? A. I said, "You came and got a bundle of mangling things of me;" she said,"Oh my God! I am sure I did not;" and I took her home.
GUILTY. Aged 16. - Confined One Month.
[trialAccount t18331128-153]
158. WILLIAM WRIGHT and EDWARD RUSSELL were indicted for feloniously assaulting John Arnet Wilmott, on the 25th of October, with intent, 1 bag, value 6d.; and 1 piece of gold, value 11l. and 2 pieces of silver, value 2l., the goods of John Harris, from the person, and against the will of the said John Arnet Wilmott, violently and feloniously to steal against the statute, &c.
2nd COUNT, stating it to be with intent to rob him.
JOHN ARNETT WILMOTT. I live at Clerkenwell, in the service of Mr. Harris. On the 25th of October, I was sent to Mr. Brown's, Clerkenwell-close, to the flatting-mills, with some gold and silver, from Mr. Harris's, in Gloucester-street - I took it there, and was returning with it, in a bag; it was about 14l. worth- it had been flattened for cases - I was going back with my mother - as I came out of Mr. Brown's, I saw the two prisoners standing at the door; as I got round the Close I saw them again, and they looked at me - they then went up a street, and I did not see them again till I got to the corner of my master's street, which is about fifty yards from Mr. Brown's - my mother then bade me good-bye - Russell then pulled my cap over my eyes, and Wright pulled my bag; but I had the string so tight, that he did not get it away; but he had the bag in his hand - my mother then came up; Russell then left my cap, and pulled at the bag - they both pulled at it, and the string cut my fingers very much, and made them bleed - I caught hold of Russell, Wright then swore at me and wanted me to fight him - my mother told me to go down the steps and tell my master - I went and gave the bag to my mistress; I then ran after the prisoners and told my mother she was to bring one of them back.
Cross-examined by MR. ADOLPHUS. Q. You knew Russell very well? A. No; I had seen him once before, but did not know where he lived - I had not played with him - I saw him once when I was looking for a place - he said to me, "Is there a boy wanted" - I did not know him name was Russell, till he told me up at the station-house - I did not know the other boy at all - they did not begin to play with me - my cap was not taken off and thrown down an area - I don't know that they knew what I had got - it was between seven and eight o'clock in the evening, on the 25th of October.
COURT. Q. Did you see them next morning? A. Yes - I saw Wright when I went to Mr. Brown's - he whistled and some more boys came to him.
MR. ADOLPHUS. Q. How long was it before this night, that you and Russell went to look for a place? A. I did not go with him, I was looking at the bill when he came up to me, and asked if a boy was wanted - I had not tole them that I had any money or property about me - they did not tell me to deliver, or that they would have my things, or anything of that kind; but one struck my cap, and the other pulled my bag, and when my mother came up, they did so still - I did not stay to hear whether my mother scolded them, they walked away talking together; my master was not at home.
COURT. Q. Did they see you come out of Brown's door? A. Yes; it is a large shop down a passage, with mills in it.
MARY ANN WILMOTT. I am the witness's mother. I went with him on the 25th of October, as he was timid - I meant to stop at the next door to his master's, that he might not know that I was with him - when I left him, he called out "Mother, they have got my cap over my eyes"- I returned back, and saw the prisoners, they had got the cap over his eyes, and were pulling the bag, and they would have got it, but the string was cat-gut - I asked them how they came to molest him; they then walked on some distance, and set their backs against a door - my boy then came, and said, I was to bring them back again; Russell said, "No, be d-d if we will; let him come to us if he wants us" - my boy said, "If you don't come to him, my master will come to you" - they then ran off laughing.
Cross-examined. Q. The first you saw was one of these boys pulling your son's cap, and the other the bag, and when they saw you they walked away? A. No, sir, they tugged a good deal - I did not call for anybody - they walked away with me, and when my boy came, and said, "My master will come to you, if you don't come to him," they went away laughing - I did not know either of them - I went towards St. John-street - I did not pass any one.
SAMUEL PARKS. I am in the service of Mr. Harris - I delivered the piece of gold to this lad to take to Mr. Brown to be flattened - it was worth upwards of 10l.; he had some silver, but that I did not give him.
JOHN FRANCIS WILMOTT. I am father of the boy. On the Sunday morning after this I took him on the ruins of Woodbridge estate, I saw one of the prisoners, and had him taken.
John Russell, Randall Chatterton, George Cox, and Benjamin Mitchell, gave Russell a good character.
George Richardson, William Dyer, and Ann Norman, gave Wright a good character.
RUSSELL - GUILTY. Aged 16.
WRIGHT. - GUILTY. Aged 15.
Recommended to Mercy by the Prosecutor and Jury - Confined Three Months.

161. THOMAS COPPIN was indicted for feloniously receiving, on the 16th of October, 3 keys, value 2s.; and 1 ring, value 1s., the goods of James Cowie, which had been lately before stolen, &c.
JAMES COWIE. I live at No. 30, St. Martin's-le-grand On the 5th of September, I lost my ring and keys in Smithfield together with my pocket handkerchief; there are the keys; they were brought to me in the middle of October, by a policeman, who said, he had found them - I know nothing of the prisoner.
WILLIAM BADCOCK (police-sergeant G 20). I took the prisoner to the station-house on the 16th of October - I went back to his house, and received these keys and ring.

162. JAMES FIELDWICK was indicted for stealing, on the 24th of May, 1 watch, value 3l.; 1 ring, value 5s.; 1 purse, value 1s.; 1 pen-knife, value 1s.; and 1 waistcoat, value 10s., the goods of George Clark; and 1 cap, value 8d., the goods of William Clark .
SARAH CLARK. I am wife of William Clark, of Turk-street, Bethnal-green; he is in the service Mr. Hanbury. On the 18th of May the prisoner came and hired a weekly lodging at my house; he lived with his mother, but slept at my house for three nights; he then absconded, and the same day, we missed the property stated from a drawer in the room he had slept in - I am sure it was all safe when he came to sleep there; we did not see him again till last Saturday week, when he was in custody; no other person could have taken the property.
Prisoner. Q. When I went down in the morning, did you not go up directly and lock the door? A. I did; I missed the cap about three hours afterwards - I have a man and his wife who have lodged with me for five years; they have not a key of the room you slept in.
COURT. Q. Did you receive the rent? A. Yes; of his father on the Monday afterwards - the prisoner gave me no notice to quit.
GEORGE CLARK. On the 24th of May; I went out, and when I returned, my sister asked me for the key of that room; she went up and came down directly, and said, she had lost her cap; I then went up and missed my watch; I have never seen it since.
WILLIAM GILLETT (police-sergeant N 19). On the 23rd of October, I took the prisoner; I said, there was a charge against him for felony in Bethnal-green; he denied all knowledge of it; I read the charge over to him, and asked if he knew a person named Clark; he said, "Yes," but denied all knowledge of the robbery.
Prisoner. I went out that day and met a young man who asked me to go into the country; I went with him, and then to some friends of mine at Royston. I staid there till my friends sent me word there was a charge against me; I came to town, went to the station-house, and asked if there was any charge against me; they said, "No;" I went home, and then the officer came after me.
GUILTY. Aged 21. - Transported for Seven Years.
[trialAccount t18331128-158]
163. JOHN JOHNSON was indicted for stealing, on the 28th of November, 28 yards of printed cotton, value 8s.; the goods of Charles Rose.
EDMUND IVE. I know Mr. Rose's shop - he is an upholsterer, and lives in King-street, Holborn. On the 28th of November between twelve and one o'clock, I saw the prisoner and two other lads pass my door, which is nearly opposite Mr. Rose's; I kept my eyes on them; the prisoner went into Mr. Rose's shop; he came out again, and spoke to his companions; they went down Holborn; the prisoner and another then came back, and made some observations on the window; they went again into Holborn, consulted there a minute or two, and came back; I then lost sight of the prisoner's companions and went down Holborn after them; I saw them go into a court, and the prisoner joined them; I took him and found this cotton on him, under his coat.
Prisoner. It was given me by a young lad, at the corner of Red Lion-street. Witness. I saw you without it, and then with it in less than ten minutes.
THOMAS BARFORD (police-constable E 110). The prisoner was given into my charge.
CHARLES ROSE. I am master of the shop. This is my property - it laid on the table for or five feet from the door, between eleven and twelve o'clock.
ELIZABETH WHITE. I work for the prosecutor. The prisoner came into the shop and asked me for a light-porter's situation; I gave him an answer, and shut the door after him; and in a few minutes the door opened, but no one came in.
J. Pearce, a butcher, gave the prisoner a good character.
GUILTY. Aged 21. Transported for Seven Years.
[trialAccount t18331128-159]
164. THOMAS DORMAN and JAMES PREDDY were indicted for stealing, on the 27th of November, 1 snuff-box, value 5s.; 1 pocket-book, value 1s. 6d.; 1 purse, value 6d.; 4 sovereigns; 2 half-sovereigns; 3 shillings, and 1 sixpence, the property of Joseph Thomas Williams, from his person.
JOSEPH THOMAS WILLIAMS. I was articled to an attorney, but am out of a situation; I have my indentures at home. On the 27th of November I was at the Hog in Armour, on Saffron-hill, between three and four o'clock in the afternoon; I was robbed of my purse, and 5l. 3s. 6d. my snuff-box, and pocket-book, by the prisoner Dorman, while I was asleep; I had not known the prisoners till I met them at the Golden Ball, in Cow-lane, about two hours before; when they came in among others, and I offered them something to drink; after drinking sometime there, they induced me to go to the Hog in Armour; when I went there, I had between 5l. and 6l. in my right-hand trowsers pocket; I was rather drunk and fell asleep; I don't know how long I slept, but when I awoke my property was gone; I told the landlord I had been robbed, and gave Dorman into custody; I saw him searched, and four sovereigns, two half-sovereigns, and 3s. 6d. were found on him; I had lost 5l. 3s. 6d., in gold, and silver; I don't know in what coin.
Dorman. When I went out, I had some money in my pocket, and this gentleman saw it. Witness. No; I did not.
DAVID REES. I am a shoemaker, and live in Tash-street, Grays-inn-lane. I was in the Hog in Armour,having a pint of porter; the prosecutor, the two prisoners, and a lad came in together; they sat down, and some beef-steaks were brought in - while they were being cooked, Preddy went and got a half-quartern loaf, and while he was out, Dorman put his hand into the prosecutor's pocket, and took out something, but I cannot tell what - he put it into his own pocket; the steaks were then brought in, and they awoke the prosecutor to eat some, and while he was eating them, he turned out his pockets and missed his money - Dorman said, while he was asleep, that he was an acquaintance of his - "In fact," says he, "he is a relation; he was an attorney's-clerk, but has lost a good situation in consequence of being intoxicated, but he knows I can get him back again" - he said, "I went to his master, and he gave me 5s. to give him" - when the prosecutor missed his money, he went to the bar, and there was a bit of a row about his losing his money, and a boy was taken into custody - I said to Dorman, "What a rascal you must be to have that boy taken;" - I then said, "That is the man you ought to take" - it was a long while before he would give him into custody.
Dorman. Q. Why did not you speak when I had my hand in his pocket? Witness. You said you was his friend.
JOHN MARSHALL. I was in the Hog in Armour, and saw these persons; the prosecutor appeared to be dozing, and Dorman put his hand into his pocket, and took it out closed - I could not see what was in it.
HUGH WILSON. I work for Dorman. He went to the Golden Ball, and fell in with the prosecutor - he drank for an hour, and then he persuaded him to go to the Hog in Armour - I was sitting next to the prosecutor, and Dorman put his hand into his pocket, and drew it out under the table, and put it into his own right-hand pocket - Preddy was then gone for bread; when he returned, he pulled me out of my sitting, and sat down there, and I saw him take the pocket-book out of the prosecutor's pocket, and some papers likewise - I was accused of being the thief, but I had nothing to do with it - when Preddy drew the papers out of the prosecutor's pocket, Dorman said, "Put them back again" - when the prosecutor missed his money, he looked very hard at me, and I was taken into custody - when Dorman came out, I said, "You are found out" - he offered me the purse, but I would not take it - Preddy put the pocketbook some where about his breast, but it has not been found.
REUBEN BENDLE (police-constable G 75). I searched the prisoner. I found four sovereigns, two half-sovereigns, and 3s. 6d. on Dorman; and this purse was close by where he stood - I asked him what money he had, and he said 2l. 17s. - I pulled it out, and said, "You have made a great mistake."
JAMES MIMMS (police-constable G 48). I assisted in taking the prisoners.
Dorman's Defence (written). On Friday, the 22nd instant, I left home with 6l. 4s. 6d. in my pocket, and met the prosecutor and some prostitutes at the Golden Ball, Cow-cross or lane. We all drank freely there, and continued drinking together at different houses all day; at length we came to the Hog in Armour, Saffron-hill, all drunk. The prosecutor had been exposing his box all day, and the prisoner fell asleep. The prosecutor was boasting how many men he had sent out of the country, when David Reece said, "We'll, send him out of the country;" and the prisoner was given in charge. The prosecutor said he would not charge the prisoner; he appeared an honest man. The girls of the town were with them all day. The purse was picked up in the street by the boy, and taken into the house. David Rees and the hackney-coachman offered to the prisoner's wife, in the presence of Mr. Parkinson, a baker, if they were paid 5s. a-piece for their time, they would get out of the way; and asked her to keep the boy back.
John Perkins, Thomas Hythe, and John Turner gave Dorman a good character.
DORMAN - GUILTY. Aged 31.
PREDDY - GUILTY. Aged 33.
Transported for Seven Years.
[trialAccount t18331128-160]
165. ROBERT BROWN was indicted for stealing, on the 20th of October, at St. Marylebone, 4 candlesticks, value 15s.; 1 mug, value 10s., the goods of George Francis Barlow - and 1 cloak, value 10s.; 1 box, value 10s.; 1 snuff-box, value 15s.; 2 caps, value 2s., and 3 £5 bank notes, the property of Ann Harris, in the dwelling-house of George Francis Barlow .
HENRY PHILLIPS. I am butler to Mr. George Francis Barlow; he keeps a house at No. 18, Bryanstone-square, in the parish of St. Marylebone. On Sunday night, the 20th of October, I was in the kitchen, and heard a noise in the housekeeper's-room, or the butler's pantry, in the lower part of the house; I went and found the prisoner lying down, concealed in the beer-cellar, with the cloak over him - I ordered him out, took him by the collar, and sent for the policeman - I had seen him at the house once before; he came to see one of the servants - he had the whole of the property on his person when I took him- these four candlesticks were in his pocket - these three £5 notes were in this box, which was locked, and he had it under his arm.
ANN HARRIS. I am housekeeper to Mr. George Francis Barlow. This box and three £5 notes, are mine, and were in the housekeeper's room on the basement story - this other property is my master's - I had seen it safe an hour before - the prisoner had been to the house to visit one of the maids.
JOHN HUNT (police-constable D 138). I was sent for, and took the prisoner - I found on him a phosphorus-box, and matches, a small chisel and a key.
Joseph Wild, and Walter Hughes, of Shepherd's-market, gave the prisoner a good character.
GUILTY. Aged 17. - Recommended to Mercy by the Prosecutor. - Transported for Life.

166. GEORGE WILLIAMS was indicted for stealing, on the 6th of September, at St. Lukes', 1 jacket, value 30s.; 1 waistcoat, value 12s.; 2 pairs of trousers, value 62s.; 4 shirts, value 8s.; 1 snuff-box, value 3s.; 1 pair of stockings, value 1s., and 1 pair of braces, value 6d., the goods of James Hucker, in his dwelling-house.
MARIA HUCKER. I am the wife of James Hucker; we live in Little Arthur-street, Goswell-street, in theparish of St. Luke. It is our dwelling-house - I had known the prisoner two months; he frequented our house to see a baker's foreman - we keep a beer-shop, and at that time we carried on the baking trade - on the 6th of September, I went out to order some beer - I left my place in care of my nephew; I left the prisoner, John Strickland, and William Church there - I returned in about three quarters of an hour - I was told the prisoner was missing; that he had gone backwards, and had not returned - I took a candle and went up-stairs; I found my box open in my front room, and the things stated were gone - I have never seen any of them since - I could not swear the box had been locked.
JOSEPH WILLEY. I live there with my aunt. The prisoner was left there; he went out two or three times, and at last he did not return - we went after him, but could not find him, but we took notice that the side-door was fast - when my aunt came home, we told her of it - she took a candle, and found the side-door open, and missed the things.
JOHN STRICKLAND. On the 6th of September, I was at the prosecutor's. I observed the prisoner go out twice, and return - he then went a third time, and did not return.
Prisoner. I went there and was taken bad in my bowels. I went to the water-closet, and then bade them good night, and went home to bed.
JURY to Willey. Q. Where is the water-closet? A. In the back yard, and there is a side-door in the passage which leads into the street - he had to pass that door.
MARIA HUCKER. The prisoner had been looked for ever since.
GUILTY. Aged 19. - Transported for Life.
[trialAccount t18331128-162]
167. JOSEPH BROUGHTON was indicted for feloniously breaking and entering the dwelling-house of William Lumley, on the 24th of November, and stealing 5 live tame fowls, value 5s., his property.
WILLIAM LUMLEY. I live in Cottage-place, City-road. It is my dwelling-house, and is in the parish of St. Luke. I keep fowls, but I was ill in bed at the time these were stolen - I know they are mine.
JOHN SAPTHORNE (police-constable G 182). On the 24th of November, I was on duty, in Goswell-street, at half-past three o'clock in the morning. I saw the prisoner with a bag in his hand - I stopped him, and asked what he had - he said, "Some fowls" - I asked him to allow me to look at them - I took them in my hand, and asked where he brought them from - he said from Finchley, and was going to take them to his brother in Old-street - I said I would go with him; I went with him to the end of Gee-street - he then ran away - I followed him - he ran on to the Willow-walk; I took him there - I asked him how many fowls there were; he said four, and he gave 4s. for them, to a man on the road.
Cross-examined by MR. STAMMERS. Q. The fowls were in a bag? A. Yes.
SARAH BUTLER. I live with Mr. Lumley my brother-in-law. There are three gates in the garden, which lead to the poultry-house, but that does not join to the dwelling-house - on Saturday week, at nine o'clock in the evening, I shut the poultry-house up secure, and these fowls were missed the next morning - I knew the prisoner - he has occasionally worked as gardener for my brother.
Cross-examined. Q. Were you in the habit of feeding them? A. Yes; very often - there was a padlock on the door of the fowl-house - the outer gate of the yard was shut the night before, but in the morning it was open.
Prisoner's Defence. I am a pork-butcher, and went down to Finchley for work, and on coming back I met a man, who said, "Will you purchase these fowls?" He asked 5s. for them, I gave him 4s.; I came on towards home, and was stopped by the policeman; I told him what I had got, and walked a few paces with him, but being agitated I ran away; I was then taken to the station-house. I was taken to the magistrate, who, having a good character of me from Mr. Stroud, and Mr. Van, the officers; gave me up, till the following Tuesday; I went to work, and was taken again.
GUILTY of Stealing only. Aged 21. - Recommended to Mercy by the Prosecutor - Confined Six Months.
[trialAccount t18331128-163]
168. ELIZABETH DIXON was indicted for assaulting John Crocker, on the 11th of November, putting him in fear, and taking from his person and against his will, 1 handkerchief, value 5s.; 1 locket, value 4s.; 1 penny; and 2 halfpence, his property.
JOHN CROCKER. I am brazier, and live in Paradise-street, Marylebone. On the 11th of November, I fell in with the prisoner, about eleven o'clock at night, in Chiswell-street; she asked me to go home with her - I was quite sober - I went to a room on the first floor where she told me she lived - we were sitting on the side of the bed; she said to me, "Tip over 2s.!" I said, "What for?" she said, "Give me the money and I tell you;" I said, "I have only one shilling, and I will give you that, for I want nothing of you;" - she made use of a bad word, told me I was a liar, and she would search me - she put her hand into both my pockets, and took out a locket, a penny-piece, and two halfpence - as soon as she took her hand from my pocket, I missed the locket; I refused to let her put her hand into my pocket, but she said, she would, and the less I said the better, as she had people about her, and I heard men's voices - I was in fear; I accused her of taking the locket; she said, she had not taken it; I said, she had as it had been in my pocket; she said, she had not got it, and I should not have it; she then asked for my handkerchief; I said, she should not have it; she snatched my hat off, and took my handkerchief out of it; I asked her for it; she said, I should not have it - she went down, and I after her; when she got near the bottom of the stairs, I heard something drop; I told her I thought something dropped; she said, no, there was not; I said, I thought there was; I called for a light, she got one from the room below, and I found the locket; she took the light into the room, and I went out and called the officer - I was not in much fear when I got down, and the door was open, I did not go out without looking for the locket, as I would run any risk for it, as it wasgiven me by a young woman; but I can swear I parted with it from fear.
STEPHEN PLANT (Police-constable). I took the prisoner; I found nothing on her - I knocked at the room-door; they would not open it; I broke it open; I found the prisoner, three girls, and two men there - I turned up the window curtains, and saw the bottom pane of glass was broken, and the paper pushed out - I went into the yard, and found this handkerchief - the prosecutor gave me this locket.
The prisoner put in a written defence, stating that the prosecutor had become angry with her, in consequence of her refusing to remain with him, and charged her with the robbery, which she positively denied.
NOT GUILTY.
[trialAccount t18331128-164]

HENRY CAMPER. I am a pawnbroker. I produce a silver spoon, and necklace, which were pawned by the prisoner, both at the same time - I knew her before, but I thought she was living with her mother.(Property produced and sworn to.)

169. THOMAS ADAMS was indicted for stealing, on the 20th of October, 1 snuff-box, value 2l., the goods of Isaac Lyon, from his person.
ISAAC LYON. I am a cheesemonger, and live in Duke-street, Aldgate. On Saturday, the 20th of October, I was in Aldgate, between two and three o'clock in the day time - I had my snuff-box in my coat-pocket; I felt a tug at my pocket, turned round, and a gentleman said, "You have been robbed, and the person who robbed you is opposite;" and pointed him out - I ran over, laid hold of the prisoner, and said, "You rascal, what have you been robbing me for," and the box fell from between his shirt and waistband; I gave him in charge - it was concealed under his waistband.
RICHARD WILLIAM PARKER (policeman). I apprehended the prisoner.(Property produced and sworn to.)
Prisoner. I picked it up.
Thomas Tilley, Baker, 19, Popham-street, Islington; - Thompson, Old-street; and William Cunningham, a sadler, gave the prisoner a good character.
